72:     return rows

73:

74: def compute\_max\_selectivity\_error(bucket\_boundaries, bucket\_counts, sample\_data):

75:     if not sample\_data or not bucket\_boundaries:

76:         return 0.0

77:     sample\_n = len(sample\_data)

78:     errors = []

79:

80:     quantiles = np.linspace(min(sample\_data), max(sample\_data), 10)

81:     for q in quantiles:

82:         actual\_sel = sum(1 for x in sample\_data if x <= q) / sample\_n

83:

84:         est\_count = 0

85:         for (low, high), cnt in zip(bucket\_boundaries, bucket\_counts):

86:             if high <= q:

87:                 est\_count += cnt

88:             elif low <= q < high:

89:                 frac = (q - low) / max(1e-6, (high - low))

90:                 est\_count += cnt * frac

91:         est\_sel = est\_count / sample\_n

92:         errors.append(abs(est\_sel - actual\_sel))

93:

94:     return max(errors) if errors else 0.0

95:

96: def run\_q2():

97:     os.makedirs('plots', exist\_ok=True)

98:     conn = psycopg2.connect(dbname='imdbload')

99:     cur = conn.cursor()

100:

101:     sample\_sizes = [1000, 3000, 5000]

102:     num\_buckets = 20

103:

104:     timings\_id = {}

105:     errors\_id = {}

106:     timings\_title = {}

107:     hist\_results = {}

108:

109:     print("=== Running Q2 Vectorized DP Experiments ===")

110:     for N in sample\_sizes:

111:         print(f"\textbackslash{}n--- Processing Sample Size N = {N} ---")

112:

113:         id\_sample = fetch\_sample(cur, N, 'id')

114:         id\_sample.sort()

115:

116:         t0 = time.time()

117:         id\_bounds, id\_counts = build\_optimal\_histogram(id\_sample, num\_buckets=num\_buckets)

118:         t1 = time.time()

119:         elapsed\_id = t1 - t0

120:         err\_id = compute\_max\_selectivity\_error(id\_bounds, id\_counts, id\_sample)

121:

122:         timings\_id[N] = elapsed\_id

123:         errors\_id[N] = err\_id

124:         print(f"[id] N={N}: Build Time = {elapsed\_id:.4f}s, Max Selectivity Error = {err\_id:.6f}")

125:

126:         title\_sample = fetch\_sample(cur, N, 'title')

127:         title\_sample.sort()

128:         title\_encoded = [string\_to\_float(t) for t in title\_sample]

129:

130:         t0 = time.time()

131:         title\_bounds\_num, title\_counts = build\_optimal\_histogram(title\_encoded, num\_buckets=num\_buckets)

132:         t1 = time.time()

133:         elapsed\_title = t1 - t0

134:         timings\_title[N] = elapsed\_title

135:         print(f"[title] N={N}: Build Time = {elapsed\_title:.4f}s")

136:

137:         if N == 3000:

138:             hist\_results['id\_3000'] = (id\_bounds, id\_counts, elapsed\_id, err\_id)

139:             hist\_results['title\_3000'] = (title\_bounds\_num, title\_counts, title\_sample, elapsed\_title)

140:

141:     id\_b\_3000, id\_c\_3000, t\_id\_3000, e\_id\_3000 = hist\_results['id\_3000']

142:     plt.figure(figsize=(10, 8))

143:     y\_labels = [f"[{int(b[0])} - {int(b[1])}]" for b in id\_b\_3000]

144:     plt.barh(range(len(id\_c\_3000)), id\_c\_3000, color='\#2e8b57', edgecolor='black', alpha=0.8)

145:     plt.yticks(range(len(id\_c\_3000)), y\_labels, fontsize=8)

146:     plt.xlabel('Frequency (X-axis)')

147:     plt.ylabel('Value Boundaries Bucket Intervals (Y-axis)')

148:     plt.title(f'Optimal Serial Histogram (B={num\_buckets}) for title.id (N=3000)')

149:     plt.grid(True, linestyle='--', alpha=0.5)

\subsubsection{4. Max Selectivity Error for Optimal Histogram (\$N=3000\$)}
-- \textbf{Max Selectivity Error}: \textbf{0.003169}

-- \textbf{Calculation}: Computed across 10 evaluation range quantiles comparing optimal bucket cumulative frequency estimation against empirical sample distribution.

+

+- \textbf{Max Selectivity Error}: \textbf{0.003169} (or \textbf{0.3169\%})

+

+\#\#\#\# A. Step-by-Step Calculation Methodology

+1. \textbf{Evaluation Quantiles (\$q\_0 \textbackslash{}dots q\_9\$)}: To systematically evaluate estimation error across the data range \$[x\_{\textbackslash{}min}, x\_{\textbackslash{}max}]\$, 10 linearly-spaced query thresholds \$q\_k\$ are evaluated:

+   \$\$q\_k = x\_{\textbackslash{}min} + k \textbackslash{}times \textbackslash{}frac{x\_{\textbackslash{}max} - x\_{\textbackslash{}min}}{9}, \textbackslash{}quad k \textbackslash{}in \textbackslash{}{0, 1, \textbackslash{}dots, 9\textbackslash{}}\$\$

+

+2. \textbf{Actual Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}(q\_k)\$)}: Computed directly from the empirical sample of \$N = 3,000\$ points:

+   \$\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}frac{\textbackslash{}text{Count}(x \textbackslash{}le q\_k)}{N}\$\$

+

+3. \textbf{Optimal Histogram Estimated Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}(q\_k)\$)}:

+   - For all buckets \$[L\_i, H\_i]\$ lying fully below \$q\_k\$ (\$H\_i \textbackslash{}le q\_k\$), add full bucket count \$n\_i\$.

+   - For the enclosing bucket (\$L\_i \textbackslash{}le q\_k < H\_i\$), perform linear interpolation:

+     \$\$\textbackslash{}text{Estimated Count } E(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}sum\_{H\_i \textbackslash{}le q\_k} n\_i + \textbackslash{}left( n\_i \textbackslash{}times \textbackslash{}frac{q\_k - L\_i}{H\_i - L\_i} \textbackslash{}right)\$\$

+   - Estimated Selectivity: \$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}frac{E(q\_k)}{N}\$

+

+4. \textbf{Maximum Selectivity Error}:

+   \$\$\textbackslash{}text{Max Selectivity Error} = \textbackslash{}max\_{k \textbackslash{}in \textbackslash{}{0..9\textbackslash{}}} |S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}(q\_k) - S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}(q\_k)| = \textbackslash{}mathbf{0.003169}\$\$

+

\subsubsection{1. Mathematical Calculation Methodology}

For \$N = 3,000\$ sample points and \$B = 20\$ optimal serial buckets \$[L\_i, H\_i]\$ with frequency counts \$n\_i\$:


1. \textbf{Evaluation Query Thresholds (\$q\_0 \textbackslash{}dots q\_9\$)}:

The data range \$[x\_{\textbackslash{}min}, x\_{\textbackslash{}max}]\$ is divided into 10 evaluation range quantiles:

\$\$q\_k = x\_{\textbackslash{}min} + k \textbackslash{}times \textbackslash{}frac{x\_{\textbackslash{}max} - x\_{\textbackslash{}min}}{9}, \textbackslash{}quad k \textbackslash{}in \textbackslash{}{0, 1, \textbackslash{}dots, 9\textbackslash{}}\$\$


2. \textbf{Empirical Actual Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}(q\_k)\$)}:

\$\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}frac{\textbackslash{}text{Count}(x \textbackslash{}le q\_k)}{N}\$\$


3. \textbf{Optimal Histogram Estimated Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}(q\_k)\$)}:

\begin{itemize}
\item For all buckets lying completely below \$q\_k\$ (\$H\_i \textbackslash{}le q\_k\$), add full bucket count \$n\_i\$.
\item For the single partial bucket enclosing \$q\_k\$ (\$L\_i \textbackslash{}le q\_k < H\_i\$), compute linear interpolation:
\$\$\textbackslash{}text{Estimated Count } E(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}sum\_{H\_i \textbackslash{}le q\_k} n\_i + \textbackslash{}left( n\_i \textbackslash{}times \textbackslash{}frac{q\_k - L\_i}{H\_i - L\_i} \textbackslash{}right)\$\$

\item Estimated Selectivity: \$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}frac{E(q\_k)}{N}\$
\end{itemize}

4. \textbf{Maximum Selectivity Error}:

\$\$\textbackslash{}text{Max Selectivity Error} = \textbackslash{}max\_{k \textbackslash{}in \textbackslash{}{0..9\textbackslash{}}} |S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}(q\_k) - S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}(q\_k)| = \textbackslash{}mathbf{0.003169} \textbackslash{}quad (\textbackslash{}mathbf{0.3169\textbackslash{}\%})\$\$


\hrule

1: import os

2: import time

3: import psycopg2

4: import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

5: import numpy as np

6:

7: def build\_optimal\_histogram(data, num\_buckets=20):

8:     n = len(data)

9:     if n == 0:

10:         return [], []

11:     if n <= num\_buckets:

12:         return [(data[i], data[i]) for i in range(n)], [1]*n

13:

14:     arr = np.array(data, dtype=np.float64)

15:     pref = np.zeros(n + 1, dtype=np.float64)

16:     pref2 = np.zeros(n + 1, dtype=np.float64)

17:     np.cumsum(arr, out=pref[1:])

18:     np.cumsum(arr**2, out=pref2[1:])

19:

20:     dp = np.full((num\_buckets + 1, n + 1), np.inf, dtype=np.float64)

21:     parent = np.zeros((num\_buckets + 1, n + 1), dtype=np.int32)

22:

23:     j\_arr = np.arange(1, n + 1)

24:     sum\_x = pref[j\_arr]

25:     sum\_x2 = pref2[j\_arr]

26:     dp[1, 1:] = np.maximum(0.0, sum\_x2 - (sum\_x**2) / j\_arr)

27:

28:     for b in range(2, num\_buckets + 1):

29:         for i in range(b, n + 1):

30:             j\_vals = np.arange(b - 1, i)

31:             sx = pref[i] - pref[j\_vals]

32:             sx2 = pref2[i] - pref2[j\_vals]

33:             cnt = i - j\_vals

34:             c\_vals = dp[b - 1, j\_vals] + np.maximum(0.0, sx2 - (sx**2) / cnt)

35:             best\_idx = np.argmin(c\_vals)

36:             dp[b, i] = c\_vals[best\_idx]

37:             parent[b, i] = j\_vals[best\_idx]

38:

39:     splits = [n]

40:     curr\_b, curr\_i = num\_buckets, n

41:     while curr\_b > 1:

42:         p = int(parent[curr\_b, curr\_i])

43:         splits.append(p)

44:         curr\_i = p

45:         curr\_b -= 1

46:     splits.append(0)

47:     splits.reverse()

48:

49:     bucket\_boundaries = []

50:     bucket\_counts = []

51:     for k in range(len(splits) - 1):

52:         s\_idx, e\_idx = splits[k], splits[k+1]

53:         bucket\_counts.append(e\_idx - s\_idx)

54:         bucket\_boundaries.append((arr[s\_idx], arr[e\_idx - 1]))

55:

56:     return bucket\_boundaries, bucket\_counts

57:

58: def string\_to\_float(s):

59:     res = 0.0

60:     for i, ch in enumerate(s[:8]):

61:         res += ord(ch) / (256 ** (i + 1))

62:     return res

63:

64: def fetch\_sample(cur, sample\_size, col\_name='id'):

65:     pct = max(0.01, (sample\_size * 100.0) / 2528312)

66:     query = f"SELECT {col\_name} FROM title TABLESAMPLE BERNOULLI({pct:.4f}) LIMIT {sample\_size};"

67:     cur.execute(query)

68:     rows = [r[0] for r in cur.fetchall()]

69:     if len(rows) < sample\_size:

70:         cur.execute(f"SELECT {col\_name} FROM title ORDER BY random() LIMIT {sample\_size};")

71:         rows = [r[0] for r in cur.fetchall()]

72:     return rows

73:

74: def compute\_max\_selectivity\_error(bucket\_boundaries, bucket\_counts, sample\_data):

75:     if not sample\_data or not bucket\_boundaries:

76:         return 0.0

77:     sample\_n = len(sample\_data)

78:     errors = []

79:

80:     quantiles = np.linspace(min(sample\_data), max(sample\_data), 10)

81:     for q in quantiles:

82:         actual\_sel = sum(1 for x in sample\_data if x <= q) / sample\_n

83:

84:         est\_count = 0

85:         for (low, high), cnt in zip(bucket\_boundaries, bucket\_counts):

86:             if high <= q:

87:                 est\_count += cnt

88:             elif low <= q < high:

89:                 frac = (q - low) / max(1e-6, (high - low))

90:                 est\_count += cnt * frac

91:         est\_sel = est\_count / sample\_n

92:         errors.append(abs(est\_sel - actual\_sel))

93:

94:     return max(errors) if errors else 0.0

95:

174: \#\#\# 7. Max Selectivity Error Calculation \& Explanation

175:

176: \#\#\#\# A. Mathematical Definition

177: - \textbf{Actual Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}\$)}: The exact fraction of rows matching the query predicate:

178:   \$\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{A}}{{N}}\$\$

179:   where \$A\$ is actual row count from \texttt{count(*)}, and \$N = {total\_title:,}\$ is total table rows.

180:

181: - \textbf{Estimated Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}\$)}: The fraction of rows predicted by PostgreSQL's query optimizer:

182:   \$\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{E}}{{N}}\$\$

183:   where \$E\$ is estimated row count from \texttt{EXPLAIN}.

184:

185: - \textbf{Absolute Selectivity Error}:

186:   \$\$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Error}} = |S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}} - S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}| = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}left|\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{E - A}}{{N}}\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}right|\$\$

187:

188: \#\#\#\# B. Step-by-Step Calculation for Evaluated Queries

189:

190: 1. \textbf{Query 1 (\texttt{title.title < 'Race'})}:

191:    - Actual Count (\$A\_1\$): \texttt{{act\_race\_cnt:,}} \$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}implies S\_{{act,1}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{{act\_race\_cnt}}}{{{total\_title}}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{{act\_race\_sel:.6f}}}\$

192:    - Estimated Count (\$E\_1\$): \texttt{{est\_race\_cnt:,}} \$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}implies S\_{{est,1}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{{est\_race\_cnt}}}{{{total\_title}}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{{est\_race\_sel:.6f}}}\$

193:    - \textbf{Absolute Error}: \$|{est\_race\_sel:.6f} - {act\_race\_sel:.6f}| = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{{err\_race:.6f}}}\$ ({err\_race*100:.4f}\% absolute error, {(err\_race/act\_race\_sel)*100:.2f}\% relative error).

194:

195: 2. \textbf{Query 2 (\texttt{title.id < 350000})}:

196:    - Actual Count (\$A\_2\$): \texttt{{act\_id\_cnt:,}} \$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}implies S\_{{act,2}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{{act\_id\_cnt}}}{{{total\_title}}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{{act\_id\_sel:.6f}}}\$

197:    - Estimated Count (\$E\_2\$): \texttt{{est\_id\_cnt:,}} \$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}implies S\_{{est,2}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{{est\_id\_cnt}}}{{{total\_title}}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{{est\_id\_sel:.6f}}}\$

198:    - \textbf{Absolute Error}: \$|{est\_id\_sel:.6f} - {act\_id\_sel:.6f}| = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{{err\_id:.6f}}}\$ ({err\_id*100:.4f}\% absolute error, {(err\_id/act\_id\_sel)*100:.2f}\% relative error).

199:

200: 3. \textbf{Max Selectivity Error}:

201:    \$\$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Max Selectivity Error}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}max(\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Error}}\_1, \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Error}}\_2) = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}max({err\_race:.6f}, {err\_id:.6f}) = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{{max\_err:.6f}}} \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}quad (\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{{max\_err*100:.4f}\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}\%}})\$\$

202:

203: \#\#\#\# C. PostgreSQL Internal Histogram Interpolation Breakdown (for \texttt{id < 350000})

204: - Search constant: \texttt{350000}.

205: - Locate enclosing bucket boundaries in \texttt{histogram\_bounds}:

206:   - \texttt{B013 = 340479}

207:   - \texttt{B014 = 366364}

208: - Value \texttt{350000} falls in \textbf{Bucket 14} (\$[340479, 366364]\$).

209: - All 13 preceding buckets are fully included: \$13 \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}times \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{1}}{{100}} = 0.130000\$.

210: - Linear interpolation inside Bucket 14:

211:   \$\$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Fraction in B14}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{350000 - 340479}}{{366364 - 340479}} \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}times \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{1}}{{100}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{9521}}{{25885}} \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}times 0.01 = 0.003678\$\$

212: - Base histogram selectivity: \$0.130000 + 0.003678 = 0.133678\$.

213: - Adjusted by non-null fraction \$(1 - \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{null\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}\_frac}})\$ yielding optimizer estimate \$E\_2 = 356,446\$ (\$S\_{{est,2}} = 0.140982\$).

214:

215: ---

216:

217: \#\#\# 8. Selectivity Estimation for Text Range Queries (\texttt{title < 'Race'})

218: For range queries on text columns:

219: 1. PostgreSQL checks if the search string lies within \texttt{pg\_stats.histogram\_bounds}.

220: 2. It identifies the lower boundary \$S\_i\$ and upper boundary \$S\_{{i+1}}\$ enclosing the search target.

221: 3. It counts all buckets strictly preceding \$S\_i\$ (\$i \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}times \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{bucket\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}\_weight}}\$).

222: 4. It computes linear interpolation within \$[S\_i, S\_{{i+1}}]\$ using prefix byte character encoding.

223: 5. The sum is multiplied by \$(1 - \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{null\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}\_frac}} - \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}sum \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{MCV\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}\_freq}})\$ to produce estimated selectivity.

224:

225: \#\#\# 9. Estimated and Actual Selectivities

226:

227: \#\#\#\# Query 1: \texttt{SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';}

228: - \textbf{Actual Row Count}: {act\_race\_cnt:,}

229: - \textbf{Actual Selectivity}: \textbf{{act\_race\_sel:.6f}} ({act\_race\_sel*100:.2f}\%)

230: - \textbf{Estimated Row Count}: {est\_race\_cnt:,}

231: - \textbf{Estimated Selectivity}: \textbf{{est\_race\_sel:.6f}} ({est\_race\_sel*100:.2f}\%)

232: - \textbf{Selectivity Error}: \textbf{{err\_race:.6f}} (Relative Error: \textbf{{(err\_race/act\_race\_sel)*100:.2f}\%})

233:

234: \#\#\#\# Query 2: \texttt{SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.id < 350000;}

235: - \textbf{Actual Row Count}: {act\_id\_cnt:,}

236: - \textbf{Actual Selectivity}: \textbf{{act\_id\_sel:.6f}} ({act\_id\_sel*100:.2f}\%)

237: - \textbf{Estimated Row Count}: {est\_id\_cnt:,}

238: - \textbf{Estimated Selectivity}: \textbf{{est\_id\_sel:.6f}} ({est\_id\_sel*100:.2f}\%)

239: - \textbf{Selectivity Error}: \textbf{{err\_id:.6f}} (Relative Error: \textbf{{(err\_id/act\_id\_sel)*100:.2f}\%})

240:

241: ---

242:

284: \#\#\# 9. Impact of Bucket Count (\$B\$) Choice

285: - \textbf{Fewer Buckets (e.g., \$B = 5\$ or \$10\$)}:

286:   - \textbf{Pros}: Lower memory footprint in system catalog, faster histogram build time (\$O(B \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}cdot N\textasciicircum{}2)\$ decreases), faster query optimization time.

287:   - \textbf{Cons}: Coarser resolution, leading to higher selectivity estimation errors, especially for skewed or non-uniform data distributions.

288: - \textbf{More Buckets (e.g., \$B = 50\$ or \$100\$)}:

289:   - \textbf{Pros}: Finer granularity, significantly lower selectivity estimation error, highly accurate cost estimation for complex query joins.

290:   - \textbf{Cons}: Higher histogram construction time, larger storage overhead in catalog, and slightly higher plan generation latency during query execution.

\subsubsection{📊 Summary of Updates Made to Question 2}

1. \textbf{Re-run Fresh Benchmark \& Sampling}:

\begin{itemize}
\item Re-executed random sampling from PostgreSQL for \$N \textbackslash{}in \textbackslash{}{1000, 3000, 5000\textbackslash{}}\$ to update all timings, bucket boundaries, frequencies, and error metrics.
\end{itemize}

2. \textbf{Q2.1 (2 Plots for \$N = 3000\$)}:

\begin{itemize}
\item Includes 2 horizontal bar plots for \$N = 3000\$: \texttt{q2\_histogram\_id\_3000.png} (\texttt{id} column) and \texttt{q2\_histogram\_title\_3000.png} (\texttt{title} column).
\end{itemize}

3. \textbf{Q2.2 (Execution Build Time for \$N = 3000\$)}:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{\texttt{id} Column}: \texttt{0.8290 seconds}
\item \textbf{\texttt{title} Column}: \texttt{0.8240 seconds}
\end{itemize}

4. \textbf{Q2.3 (Frequency Bucket Boundaries for \$N = 3000\$)}:

\begin{itemize}
\item Listed 20 exact value interval boundaries and row frequency counts (\$n\_i\$) for both \texttt{id} and \texttt{title} from the fresh run.
\end{itemize}

5. \textbf{Q2.4 (Max Selectivity Error for \$N = 3000\$)}:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Max Selectivity Error}: \texttt{0.003720} (or \texttt{0.3720\%}).
\item Includes full 10-quantile evaluation table (\$q\_0 \textbackslash{}dots q\_9\$).
\end{itemize}

6. \textbf{Q2.5 (4 Plots total: 2 for \$N = 1000\$ and 2 for \$N = 3000\$)}:

\begin{itemize}
\item Included 4 plot visualizations:
\item \texttt{q2\_histogram\_id\_1000.png} (\$N=1000\$ \texttt{id})
\item \texttt{q2\_histogram\_title\_1000.png} (\$N=1000\$ \texttt{title})
\item \texttt{q2\_histogram\_id\_3000.png} (\$N=3000\$ \texttt{id})
\item \texttt{q2\_histogram\_title\_3000.png} (\$N=3000\$ \texttt{title})
\item Included timing/error comparison table for \$N \textbackslash{}in \textbackslash{}{1000, 3000, 5000\textbackslash{}}\$.
\end{itemize}

7. \textbf{Q2.6 (Full Step-by-Step Linear Regression Derivation)}:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Sample Points \$(N\_i, T\_i)\$}: \$(1000, 0.1994\textbackslash{}text{s})\$, \$(3000, 0.8290\textbackslash{}text{s})\$, \$(5000, 2.1767\textbackslash{}text{s})\$
\item \textbf{Means}: \$\textbackslash{}bar{N} = 3000\$, \$\textbackslash{}bar{T} = 1.06837\textbackslash{}text{s}\$
\item \textbf{Sum of Squared \$N\$ Deviations}: \$\textbackslash{}sum (N\_i - \textbackslash{}bar{N})\textasciicircum{}2 = 8,000,000\$
\item \textbf{Sum of Cross Products}: \$\textbackslash{}sum (N\_i - \textbackslash{}bar{N})(T\_i - \textbackslash{}bar{T}) = 3954.72\$
\item \textbf{Calculated Slope (\$m\$)}: \$m = \textbackslash{}frac{3954.72}{8,000,000} = \textbackslash{}mathbf{0.00049434 \textbackslash{}text{ s/sample}}\$
\item \textbf{Calculated Intercept (\$c\$)}: \$c = 1.06837 - (0.00049434 \textbackslash{}times 3000) = \textbackslash{}mathbf{-0.414643 \textbackslash{}text{ s}}\$
\item \textbf{Extrapolated Full Table Time (\$N = 2,528,312\$)}:
\$\$T(2,528,312) = 0.00049434 \textbackslash{}times 2,528,312 - 0.414643 = \textbackslash{}mathbf{1249.44 \textbackslash{}text{ seconds}} \textbackslash{}quad (\textbackslash{}mathbf{20.82 \textbackslash{}text{ minutes}})\$\$

\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{AI ASSISTANT RESPONSE}

The table layout issue in \texttt{Assignment\_Report.pdf} has been fixed and pushed to GitHub!


\hrule


\subsubsection{🛠️ What Was Causing the Overflow \& Squishing:}
1. \textbf{Hardcoded Unwrapped Left Columns (\texttt{l})}: Previously, columns were specified as \texttt{l} (left-aligned without text wrapping). For wide tables with 6 or 7 columns (like the Quantile Evaluation Table), numeric values like \texttt{1,965,826.1} and \texttt{0.781333} forced LaTeX to expand those columns to their full un-wrapped length. This squished column 2 (\texttt{Query Threshold (\$q\_k\$)}) into a tiny vertical strip and pushed columns 6 \& 7 past the right page margin.


\hrule


\subsubsection{💡 Fix Applied:}
1. \textbf{Custom Flexible Column Type (\texttt{C})}: Defined a centered flexible column type \texttt{\textbackslash{}newcolumntype{C}{>{\textbackslash{}centering\textbackslash{}arraybackslash}X}} in the LaTeX template.

2. \textbf{Proportional Column Widths}:

\begin{itemize}
\item For wide tables (\$\textbackslash{}ge 6\$ columns), all data columns now use \texttt{C} flexible widths (\texttt{C | C | C | C | C | C | C}), guaranteeing that every column receives a proportional width within the page boundaries.
\item Column titles like "Query Threshold (\$q\_k\$)" and "Actual Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}\$)" wrap cleanly across 2 lines.
3. \textbf{Table Spacing \& Margins}:

\item Set page margins to \texttt{0.75in}.
\item Adjusted column padding (\texttt{\textbackslash{}tabcolsep{3.5pt}}) and font size (\texttt{\textbackslash{}footnotesize}) for wide tables so they fit 100\% inside page margins without any horizontal scrolling or cutoff.
\end{itemize}

\hrule

48: \# Assignment 1: Histogram

49:

50: \#\# Q1. Histogram Analysis for \texttt{id} and \texttt{title} Columns of \texttt{title} Table

51:

52: \#\#\# 1. Corresponding SQL Query

53: to see what all colums needed form `pg\_stats'

54: \texttt{}`sql

55: \textbackslash{}d pg\_stats;

56: \texttt{}`

57: to see histogram boundary values and other relevent  stored in \texttt{pg\_stats}:

58: \texttt{}`sql

59: SELECT

60:     attname,

61:     null\_frac,

62:     n\_distinct,

63:     most\_common\_vals,

64:     most\_common\_freqs,

65:     histogram\_bounds

66: FROM pg\_stats

67: WHERE tablename = 'title'

68:   AND attname IN ('id', 'title');

69: \texttt{}`

70:

71: \#\#\# 2. Kind of Histogram

72: PSQL uses an Equi Depth Histogram combined with a Most Common Values list. In an equidepth histogram, all buckets contain approximately the same number of rows (\$approx N / B\$), while bucket boundaries vary in width according to data distribution

73:

74: \#\#\# 3. Number of Buckets

75: - Column \texttt{id}: \textbf{100 buckets} (101 bucket boundary values)

76: - Column \texttt{title}: \textbf{100 buckets} (101 bucket boundary values)

77: - extra -  to see the defualt number of buckets

78: \texttt{}`sql

79: SHOW default\_statistics\_target;

80: \texttt{}`

81:

82: \#\#\# 4. How Textual Attribute \texttt{title} is Put into Histogram

83: textual attribute “title” is put into histogram as:

84: 1. sorting the text strings in lexicographical order like bannana and apple will be apple, bannana after sorting

85: 2. Sampling string values from the table like table title has around 2.5M rows , it will only sample around 30K

86: 3. Encoding initial string prefix bytes into floating-point numbers in \$[0.0, 1.0]\$ range for quantitative interpolation like laptop will be broken to lap with l = 108, a=97, p=112 then by 256 as between 0 and 1 , lap will be around 0.42 , we can then see where a key belongs between two keeys

87: 4. Selecting quantile boundary string values at equal percentile intervals to form the 100 equi-depth bucket boundaries stored in \texttt{pg\_stats.histogram\_bounds}.

88:

306: \#\#\# 7. Max Selectivity Error

307: - \textbf{Max Selectivity Error}: Since \texttt{default\_statistics\_target} is 100, there are 100 buckets. Therefore, the maximum selectivity error for a range query is bounded by:

308: \$\$\textbackslash{}text{Max Error} \textbackslash{}le \textbackslash{}frac{1}{B} = \textbackslash{}frac{1}{100} = 0.01 \textbackslash{}quad (1.0\textbackslash{}\%)\$\$

309: For evaluated queries(q9), the observed maximum selectivity error is \textbf{0.8364\%} (0.008364), which strictly satisfies \$\textbackslash{}text{Max Error} \textbackslash{}le 1.0\textbackslash{}\%\$.

310:

311: ---

312: \#\#\# 8: Selectivity Estimation for Text Columns

313: Selectivity estimate is done for range query on text column such as “title” in PSQL such as:

314: *The engine scans the \texttt{histogram\_bounds} array stored under pg\_stats to locate the specific bucket where the target string  is, after that it counts all complete buckets preceding the target bucket. Each full bucket represents a fixed fraction of total rows (1/B). For the target bucket, PostgreSQL converts initial string bytes into floating-point numbers in \$[0.0, 1.0]\$ range for interpolation as explain in part 4,  assuming uniform distribution inside the target bucket, it calculates the target's position using:

315:

316: \$\$\textbackslash{}text{Fraction} = \textbackslash{}frac{\textbackslash{}text{Query Value} - \textbackslash{}text{Bucket Min}}{\textbackslash{}text{Bucket Max} - \textbackslash{}text{Bucket Min}}\$\$

317: *   Final Calculation is done by adding the fractional bucket estimate to the sum of preceding full buckets to get the final estimated selectivity.

318:

319: \#\#\# 9: Estimated vs. Actual Selectivities

320:

321: To calculate estimated and actual selectivities of the queries and moreover there accuracy we compare estimated rows (from \texttt{EXPLAIN}) against actual rows (from \texttt{EXPLAIN ANALYZE}).

322: Total row count (\$N\$) for the \texttt{title} table is 2,528,312.

323:

324: \textbf{Query 1: \texttt{title < 'Race'}}

325: \texttt{}`sql

326: EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';

327: \texttt{}`

329:

330: \texttt{}`sql

331: EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';

332: \texttt{}`

333: - \textbf{Estimated Selectivity:} The planner estimated 1,939,288 rows: \$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}} = \textbackslash{}frac{1939288}{2528312} \textbackslash{}approx 0.7670\$ (76.70\%).

334:

335: - \textbf{Observation:} The absolute error is roughly 0.83\%.

417: \#\#\# 4. Max Selectivity Error for Optimal Histogram (\$N=3000\$)

418:

419: - \textbf{Max Selectivity Error}: \textbf{0.003720} (or \textbf{0.3720\%})

420:

421: \#\#\#\# Theoretical error , when worst case senario occurs ie it is skewed heavily

422: \$\$\textbackslash{}text{Max Error (Theoretical)} = \textbackslash{}frac{\textbackslash{}max(C\_i)}{N}\$\$

423:

424: \#\#\#\# A. Step-by-Step Calculation Methodology

425: 1. \textbf{Evaluation Quantiles (\$q\_0 \textbackslash{}dots q\_9\$)}: To systematically evaluate estimation error across the data range \$[x\_{\textbackslash{}min}, x\_{\textbackslash{}max}]\$, 10 linearly-spaced query thresholds \$q\_k\$ are evaluated:

426:    \$\$q\_k = x\_{\textbackslash{}min} + k \textbackslash{}times \textbackslash{}frac{x\_{\textbackslash{}max} - x\_{\textbackslash{}min}}{9}, \textbackslash{}quad k \textbackslash{}in \textbackslash{}{0, 1, \textbackslash{}dots, 9\textbackslash{}}\$\$

427:

428: 2. \textbf{Actual Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}(q\_k)\$)}: Computed directly from the empirical sample of \$N = 3,000\$ points:

429:    \$\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}frac{\textbackslash{}text{Count}(x \textbackslash{}le q\_k)}{N}\$\$

430:

431: 3. \textbf{Optimal Histogram Estimated Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}(q\_k)\$)}:

432:    - For all buckets \$[L\_i, H\_i]\$ lying fully below \$q\_k\$ (\$H\_i \textbackslash{}le q\_k\$), add full bucket count \$n\_i\$.

433:    - For the enclosing bucket (\$L\_i \textbackslash{}le q\_k < H\_i\$), perform linear interpolation:

434:      \$\$\textbackslash{}text{Estimated Count } E(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}sum\_{H\_i \textbackslash{}le q\_k} n\_i + \textbackslash{}left( n\_i \textbackslash{}times \textbackslash{}frac{q\_k - L\_i}{H\_i - L\_i} \textbackslash{}right)\$\$

435:    - Estimated Selectivity: \$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}frac{E(q\_k)}{N}\$

436:

437: 4. \textbf{Maximum Selectivity Error}:

438:    \$\$\textbackslash{}text{Max Selectivity Error} = \textbackslash{}max\_{k \textbackslash{}in \textbackslash{}{0..9\textbackslash{}}} |S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}(q\_k) - S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}(q\_k)| = \textbackslash{}mathbf{0.003720}\$\$

439:

440: \#\#\#\# B. Quantile-by-Quantile Error Evaluation Table (\$N=3000, B=20\$)

441: | Index (\$k\$) | Query Threshold (\$q\_k\$) | Actual Count (\$A\$) | Actual Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}\$) | Estimated Count (\$E\$) | Estimated Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}\$) | Absolute Error \$\textbackslash{}vert S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}} - S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}} \textbackslash{}vert\$ |

442: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |

443: | \$q\_0\$ | \texttt{257.0} | 1 | 0.000333 | 0.00 | 0.000000 | 0.000333 |

444: | \$q\_1\$ | \texttt{281,123.3} | 342 | 0.114000 | 337.42 | 0.112473 | 0.001527 |

445: | \$q\_2\$ | \texttt{561,989.7} | 668 | 0.222667 | 674.82 | 0.224941 | 0.002274 |

451: | \$q\_8\$ | \texttt{2,247,187.7} | 2669 | 0.889667 | 2664.34 | 0.888114 | 0.001553 |

476:

477: \#\#\# 6. Full Step-by-Step Linear Regression Extrapolation (\$N = 2,528,312\$)

478:

479: To extrapolate the construction build time for the entire \texttt{title} table (\$N = 2,528,312\$), we fit a linear regression model:

480: \$\$T(N) = m \textbackslash{}cdot N + c\$\$

481:

482: \#\#\#\# A. Sample Data Points \$(N\_i, T\_i)\$

483: - Point 1: \$(N\_1, T\_1) = (1000, 0.1994)\$

484: - Point 2: \$(N\_2, T\_2) = (3000, 0.8290)\$

485: - Point 3: \$(N\_3, T\_3) = (5000, 2.1767)\$

486:

487: \#\#\#\# B. Mean Calculations

488: - \$\textbackslash{}bar{N} = \textbackslash{}frac{1000 + 3000 + 5000}{3} = \textbackslash{}mathbf{3000}\$

489: - \$\textbackslash{}bar{T} = \textbackslash{}frac{0.1994 + 0.8290 + 2.1767}{3} = \textbackslash{}mathbf{1.0684\textbackslash{}text{ seconds}}\$

490:

491: \#\#\#\# C. Deviations \& Sum of Products

492: | \$N\_i\$ | \$T\_i\$ (s) | \$(N\_i - \textbackslash{}bar{N})\$ | \$(T\_i - \textbackslash{}bar{T})\$ | \$(N\_i - \textbackslash{}bar{N})\textasciicircum{}2\$ | \$(N\_i - \textbackslash{}bar{N})(T\_i - \textbackslash{}bar{T})\$ |

493: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |

494: | 1000 | 0.1994 | -2000 | -0.8690 | 4,000,000 | 1738.0037 |

495: | 3000 | 0.8290 | 0 | -0.2394 | 0 | -0.0000 |

496: | 5000 | 2.1767 | +2000 | 1.1084 | 4,000,000 | 2216.7320 |

497: | \textbf{Sum} | | | | \textbf{8,000,000} | \textbf{3954.7358} |

498:

499: \#\#\#\# D. Slope (\$m\$) and Intercept (\$c\$) Formulas

500: - \textbf{Slope (\$m\$)}:

501:   \$\$m = \textbackslash{}frac{\textbackslash{}sum (N\_i - \textbackslash{}bar{N})(T\_i - \textbackslash{}bar{T})}{\textbackslash{}sum (N\_i - \textbackslash{}bar{N})\textasciicircum{}2} = \textbackslash{}frac{3954.7358}{8,000,000} = \textbackslash{}mathbf{0.00049434\textbackslash{}text{ seconds / sample}}\$\$

502:

503: - \textbf{Intercept (\$c\$)}:

504:   \$\$c = \textbackslash{}bar{T} - m \textbackslash{}cdot \textbackslash{}bar{N} = 1.0684 - (0.00049434 \textbackslash{}times 3000) = \textbackslash{}mathbf{-0.414643\textbackslash{}text{ seconds}}\$\$

505:

506: \#\#\#\# E. Extrapolated Build Time for Full Table (\$N = 2,528,312\$)

507: \$\$T(2,528,312) = (0.00049434 \textbackslash{}times 2,528,312) + (-0.414643) = \textbackslash{}mathbf{1249.44\textbackslash{}text{ seconds}} \textbackslash{}quad (\textbackslash{}mathbf{20.82\textbackslash{}text{ minutes}})\$\$

508:

509: ---

510:

511: \#\#\# 7. Sample Size vs Time Relationship Plot

512: ![Sample Size vs Time](../plots/q2\_sample\_size\_vs\_time.png)

513:

514: ---

515:

516: \#\#\# 8. Sample Size vs Max Selectivity Error Relationship Plot

517: ![Sample Size vs Max Selectivity Error](../plots/q2\_sample\_size\_vs\_error.png)

518:

519: ---

520:

521: \#\# 9: The Trade-off of Bucket Counts

522:

523: When building a histogram, choosing the number of buckets (\$B\$) is a classic trade off between performance and accuracy.

524:

525: \textbf{Choosing Fewer Buckets (e.g., 5 to 10)}

526: If we use a small number of buckets, the biggest advantage is speed and efficiency. The histogram takes up very little memory in the system catalog and builds much faster because the time complexity of our algorithm, \$O(B \textbackslash{}cdot N\textasciicircum{}2)\$, drops significantly. The query planner also spends less time analyzing it. However, the major downside is that we lose a lot of data resolution. Because the buckets are so wide, the internal mathematical estimates become much less accurate, especially if the data is heavily skewed or unevenly distributed.

527:

528: \textbf{Choosing More Buckets (e.g., 50 to 100)}

529: On the flip side, using a large number of buckets gives the database a high-definition view of how the data is distributed. The estimates become incredibly precise, which leads to much lower selectivity errors and helps the query optimizer make smarter decisions for complex joins. The catch is that this accuracy comes at a heavy system cost. It takes noticeably longer to calculate and construct the histogram, consumes more storage space, and adds a slight delay to the query planning phase before execution can actually begin.

1: import os

2: import time

3: import psycopg2

4: import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

5: import numpy as np

6:

7: def build\_optimal\_histogram(data, num\_buckets=20):

8:     n = len(data)

9:     if n == 0:

10:         return [], []

11:     if n <= num\_buckets:

12:         return [(data[i], data[i]) for i in range(n)], [1]*n

13:

14:     arr = np.array(data, dtype=np.float64)

15:     pref = np.zeros(n + 1, dtype=np.float64)

16:     pref2 = np.zeros(n + 1, dtype=np.float64)

17:     np.cumsum(arr, out=pref[1:])

18:     np.cumsum(arr**2, out=pref2[1:])

19:

20:     dp = np.full((num\_buckets + 1, n + 1), np.inf, dtype=np.float64)

21:     parent = np.zeros((num\_buckets + 1, n + 1), dtype=np.int32)

22:

23:     j\_arr = np.arange(1, n + 1)

24:     sum\_x = pref[j\_arr]

25:     sum\_x2 = pref2[j\_arr]

26:     dp[1, 1:] = np.maximum(0.0, sum\_x2 - (sum\_x**2) / j\_arr)

27:

28:     for b in range(2, num\_buckets + 1):

29:         for i in range(b, n + 1):

30:             j\_vals = np.arange(b - 1, i)

31:             sx = pref[i] - pref[j\_vals]

32:             sx2 = pref2[i] - pref2[j\_vals]

33:             cnt = i - j\_vals

34:             c\_vals = dp[b - 1, j\_vals] + np.maximum(0.0, sx2 - (sx**2) / cnt)

35:             best\_idx = np.argmin(c\_vals)

36:             dp[b, i] = c\_vals[best\_idx]

37:             parent[b, i] = j\_vals[best\_idx]

38:

39:     splits = [n]

40:     curr\_b, curr\_i = num\_buckets, n

41:     while curr\_b > 1:

42:         p = int(parent[curr\_b, curr\_i])

43:         splits.append(p)

44:         curr\_i = p

45:         curr\_b -= 1

46:     splits.append(0)

47:     splits.reverse()

48:

49:     bucket\_boundaries = []

50:     bucket\_counts = []

51:     for k in range(len(splits) - 1):

52:         s\_idx, e\_idx = splits[k], splits[k+1]

53:         bucket\_counts.append(e\_idx - s\_idx)

54:         bucket\_boundaries.append((arr[s\_idx], arr[e\_idx - 1]))

55:

56:     return bucket\_boundaries, bucket\_counts

57:

58: def string\_to\_float(s):

59:     res = 0.0

60:     for i, ch in enumerate(s[:8]):

61:         res += ord(ch) / (256 ** (i + 1))

62:     return res

64: def fetch\_sample(cur, sample\_size, col\_name='id'):

65:     cur.execute("SELECT setseed(0.42);")

66:     pct = max(0.01, (sample\_size * 100.0) / 2528312)

67:     query = f"SELECT {col\_name} FROM title TABLESAMPLE BERNOULLI({pct:.4f}) LIMIT {sample\_size};"

68:     cur.execute(query)

69:     rows = [r[0] for r in cur.fetchall()]

70:     if len(rows) < sample\_size:

71:         cur.execute("SELECT setseed(0.42);")

72:         cur.execute(f"SELECT {col\_name} FROM title ORDER BY random() LIMIT {sample\_size};")

73:         rows = [r[0] for r in cur.fetchall()]

74:     return rows

75:

76: def compute\_max\_selectivity\_error(bucket\_boundaries, bucket\_counts, sample\_data):

77:     if not sample\_data or not bucket\_boundaries:

78:         return 0.0

79:     sample\_n = len(sample\_data)

80:     errors = []

81:

82:     quantiles = np.linspace(min(sample\_data), max(sample\_data), 10)

83:     for q in quantiles:

84:         actual\_sel = sum(1 for x in sample\_data if x <= q) / sample\_n

85:

86:         est\_count = 0

87:         for (low, high), cnt in zip(bucket\_boundaries, bucket\_counts):

88:             if high <= q:

89:                 est\_count += cnt

90:             elif low <= q < high:

91:                 frac = (q - low) / max(1e-6, (high - low))

92:                 est\_count += cnt * frac

93:         est\_sel = est\_count / sample\_n

94:         errors.append(abs(est\_sel - actual\_sel))

95:

96:     return max(errors) if errors else 0.0

97:

98: def generate\_histogram\_plot(bounds, counts, sample\_data, title\_str, save\_path, is\_string=False):

99:     plt.figure(figsize=(10, 8))

100:     labels = []

101:     if is\_string:

102:         cum\_cnt = 0

103:         for cnt in counts:

104:             start\_str = sample\_data[cum\_cnt][:15]

105:             cum\_cnt += cnt

106:             end\_str = sample\_data[cum\_cnt - 1][:15]

107:             labels.append(f"['{start\_str}' - '{end\_str}']")

108:         color\_choice = '\#4682b4'

109:         ylabel\_str = 'Value Boundaries String Buckets (Y-axis)'

110:     else:

111:         labels = [f"[{int(b[0])} - {int(b[1])}]" for b in bounds]

112:         color\_choice = '\#2e8b57'

113:         ylabel\_str = 'Value Boundaries Bucket Intervals (Y-axis)'

114:

115:     plt.barh(range(len(counts)), counts, color=color\_choice, edgecolor='black', alpha=0.8)

116:     plt.yticks(range(len(counts)), labels, fontsize=8)

117:     plt.xlabel('Frequency (X-axis)')

118:     plt.ylabel(ylabel\_str)

119:     plt.title(title\_str)

120:     plt.grid(True, linestyle='--', alpha=0.5)

121:     plt.tight\_layout()

122:     plt.savefig(save\_path, dpi=300)

123:     plt.close()

125: def run\_q2():

126:     os.makedirs('plots', exist\_ok=True)

127:     conn = psycopg2.connect(dbname='imdbload')

128:     cur = conn.cursor()

129:

133:     timings\_id = {}

134:     errors\_id = {}

135:     timings\_title = {}

138:     print("=== Running Q2 Vectorized DP Experiments ===")

147:         t1 = time.time()

150:

151:         timings\_id[N] = elapsed\_id

152:         errors\_id[N] = err\_id

153:         print(f"[id] N={N}: Build Time = {elapsed\_id:.4f}s, Max Selectivity Error = {err\_id:.6f}")

154:

155:         title\_sample = fetch\_sample(cur, N, 'title')

156:         title\_sample.sort()

157:         title\_encoded = [string\_to\_float(t) for t in title\_sample]

158:

159:         t0 = time.time()

160:         title\_bounds\_num, title\_counts = build\_optimal\_histogram(title\_encoded, num\_buckets=num\_buckets)

161:         t1 = time.time()

162:         elapsed\_title = t1 - t0

163:         timings\_title[N] = elapsed\_title

164:         print(f"[title] N={N}: Build Time = {elapsed\_title:.4f}s")

165:

166:         hist\_results[N] = {

167:             'id\_bounds': id\_bounds,

168:             'id\_counts': id\_counts,

169:             'id\_sample': id\_sample,

170:             'elapsed\_id': elapsed\_id,

171:             'err\_id': err\_id,

172:             'title\_bounds\_num': title\_bounds\_num,

173:             'title\_counts': title\_counts,

174:             'title\_sample': title\_sample,

175:             'elapsed\_title': elapsed\_title

176:         }

177:

178:         \# Generate plots for N=1000 and N=3000

179:         if N in [1000, 3000]:

180:             generate\_histogram\_plot(

181:                 id\_bounds, id\_counts, id\_sample,

182:                 f'Optimal Serial Histogram (B={num\_buckets}) for title.id (N={N})',

183:                 f'plots/q2\_histogram\_id\_{N}.png',

184:                 is\_string=False

185:             )

187:                 title\_bounds\_num, title\_counts, title\_sample,

188:                 f'Optimal Serial Histogram (B={num\_buckets}) for title.title (N={N})',

189:                 f'plots/q2\_histogram\_title\_{N}.png',

190:                 is\_string=True

191:             )

48: \# Assignment 1: Histogram

49:

50: \#\# Q1. Histogram Analysis for \texttt{id} and \texttt{title} Columns of \texttt{title} Table

51:

52: \#\#\# 1. Corresponding SQL Query

53: to see what all colums needed form `pg\_stats'

54: \texttt{}`sql

55: \textbackslash{}d pg\_stats;

56: \texttt{}`

57: to see histogram boundary values and other relevent  stored in \texttt{pg\_stats}:

58: \texttt{}`sql

59: SELECT

60:     attname,

61:     null\_frac,

62:     n\_distinct,

63:     most\_common\_vals,

64:     most\_common\_freqs,

65:     histogram\_bounds

66: FROM pg\_stats

67: WHERE tablename = 'title'

68:   AND attname IN ('id', 'title');

69: \texttt{}`

70:

71: \#\#\# 2. Kind of Histogram

72: PSQL uses an Equi Depth Histogram combined with a Most Common Values list. In an equidepth histogram, all buckets contain approximately the same number of rows (\$approx N / B\$), while bucket boundaries vary in width according to data distribution

73:

74: \#\#\# 3. Number of Buckets

75: - Column \texttt{id}: \textbf{100 buckets} (101 bucket boundary values)

76: - Column \texttt{title}: \textbf{100 buckets} (101 bucket boundary values)

77: - extra -  to see the defualt number of buckets

78: \texttt{}`sql

79: SHOW default\_statistics\_target;

80: \texttt{}`

81:

82: \#\#\# 4. How Textual Attribute \texttt{title} is Put into Histogram

83: textual attribute “title” is put into histogram as:

84: 1. sorting the text strings in lexicographical order like bannana and apple will be apple, bannana after sorting

85: 2. Sampling string values from the table like table title has around 2.5M rows , it will only sample around 30K

86: 3. Encoding initial string prefix bytes into floating-point numbers in \$[0.0, 1.0]\$ range for quantitative interpolation like laptop will be broken to lap with l = 108, a=97, p=112 then by 256 as between 0 and 1 , lap will be around 0.42 , we can then see where a key belongs between two keeys

87: 4. Selecting quantile boundary string values at equal percentile intervals to form the 100 equi-depth bucket boundaries stored in \texttt{pg\_stats.histogram\_bounds}.

88:

89: \#\#\# 5. Exact Bucket Boundaries List (From \texttt{pg\_stats})

90:

301: \#\#\# 6. Histogram Visualization

302: ![Histogram for title.id](../plots/q1\_histogram\_id.png)

306: \#\#\# 7. Max Selectivity Error

307: - \textbf{Max Selectivity Error}: Since \texttt{default\_statistics\_target} is 100, there are 100 buckets. Therefore, the maximum selectivity error for a range query is bounded by:

308: \$\$\textbackslash{}text{Max Error} \textbackslash{}le \textbackslash{}frac{1}{B} = \textbackslash{}frac{1}{100} = 0.01 \textbackslash{}quad (1.0\textbackslash{}\%)\$\$

309: For evaluated queries(q9), the observed maximum selectivity error is \textbf{0.8364\%} (0.008364), which strictly satisfies \$\textbackslash{}text{Max Error} \textbackslash{}le 1.0\textbackslash{}\%\$.

310:

311: ---

312: \#\#\# 8: Selectivity Estimation for Text Columns

313: Selectivity estimate is done for range query on text column such as “title” in PSQL such as:

314: *The engine scans the \texttt{histogram\_bounds} array stored under pg\_stats to locate the specific bucket where the target string  is, after that it counts all complete buckets preceding the target bucket. Each full bucket represents a fixed fraction of total rows (1/B). For the target bucket, PostgreSQL converts initial string bytes into floating-point numbers in \$[0.0, 1.0]\$ range for interpolation as explain in part 4,  assuming uniform distribution inside the target bucket, it calculates the target's position using:

315:

316: \$\$\textbackslash{}text{Fraction} = \textbackslash{}frac{\textbackslash{}text{Query Value} - \textbackslash{}text{Bucket Min}}{\textbackslash{}text{Bucket Max} - \textbackslash{}text{Bucket Min}}\$\$

317: *   Final Calculation is done by adding the fractional bucket estimate to the sum of preceding full buckets to get the final estimated selectivity.

318:

319: \#\#\# 9: Estimated vs. Actual Selectivities

320:

321: To calculate estimated and actual selectivities of the queries and moreover there accuracy we compare estimated rows (from \texttt{EXPLAIN}) against actual rows (from \texttt{EXPLAIN ANALYZE}).

322: Total row count (\$N\$) for the \texttt{title} table is 2,528,312.

323:

324: \textbf{Query 1: \texttt{title < 'Race'}}

325: \texttt{}`sql

326: EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';

327: \texttt{}`

328: - \textbf{Actual Selectivity:} The query physically returned 1,918,141 matching rows: \$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}} = \textbackslash{}frac{1918141}{2528312} \textbackslash{}approx 0.7587\$ (75.87\%).

329:

330: \texttt{}`sql

331: EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';

332: \texttt{}`

333: - \textbf{Estimated Selectivity:} The planner estimated 1,939,288 rows: \$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}} = \textbackslash{}frac{1939288}{2528312} \textbackslash{}approx 0.7670\$ (76.70\%).

334:

335: - \textbf{Observation:} The absolute error is roughly 0.83\%.

336:

337: \textbf{Query 2: \texttt{id < 350000}}

338: \texttt{}`sql

339: EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.id < '350000';

340: \texttt{}`

341: - \textbf{Actual Selectivity:} The query returned 349,999 matching rows: \$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}} = \textbackslash{}frac{349999}{2528312} \textbackslash{}approx 0.1384\$ (13.84\%).

342:

343: \texttt{}`sql

344: EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.id < '350000';

345: \texttt{}`

346: - \textbf{Estimated Selectivity:} The planner estimated 337,982 rows: \$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}} = \textbackslash{}frac{337982}{2528312} \textbackslash{}approx 0.1337\$ (13.37\%).

347:

348: - \textbf{Observation:} The absolute error here is about 0.47\%.

349:

350: ---

351:

352: \#\# Q2. Optimal Serial Histogram Construction (\$B=20\$ Buckets)

353:

354: \#\#\# 1. Histogram Visualizations for \$N = 3000\$ Samples (Horizontal Bar Plots)

355: *As specified in ques the histograms as a bar-plot with value boundaries in the Y-axis and

356: frequency in the X-axis*

357:

358: ![Optimal Serial Histogram for title.id (N=3000)](../plots/q2\_histogram\_id\_3000.png)

359:

360: ![Optimal Serial Histogram for title.title (N=3000)](../plots/q2\_histogram\_title\_3000.png)

361:

362: \#\#\# 2. Execution Build Time

363: - \textbf{Execution Build Time for \$N=3000\$}: \textbf{0.8290 seconds} (\texttt{id} column) / \textbf{0.8240 seconds} (\texttt{title} column)

364:

365: \#\#\# 3. Frequency Bucket Boundaries (\$N=3000\$, \$B=20\$)

366:

367: The optimal serial histogram algorithm partitions the 3,000 samples into 20 dynamic buckets to minimize total Sum of Squared Errors . The exact bucket intervals and row frequency counts (\$n\_i\$) for both columns are listed below:

368:

369: \#\#\#\# A. Column \texttt{id} Frequency Bucket Boundaries (\$N=3000\$, \$B=20\$)

370: | Bucket \# | Value Interval (\texttt{id}) | Frequency Count (\$n\_i\$) |

371: | :---: | :---: | :---: |

372: | Bucket 01 | [\texttt{257} - \texttt{118,070}] | 150 |

416:

417: \#\#\# 4. Max Selectivity Error for Optimal Histogram (\$N=3000\$)

418:

419: - \textbf{Max Selectivity Error}: \textbf{0.003720} (or \textbf{0.3720\%})

420:

421: \#\#\#\# Theoretical error , when worst case senario occurs ie it is skewed heavily

422: \$\$\textbackslash{}text{Max Error (Theoretical)} = \textbackslash{}frac{\textbackslash{}max(C\_i)}{N}\$\$

423:

424: \#\#\#\# A. Step-by-Step Calculation Methodology

425: 1. \textbf{Evaluation Quantiles (\$q\_0 \textbackslash{}dots q\_9\$)}: To systematically evaluate estimation error across the data range \$[x\_{\textbackslash{}min}, x\_{\textbackslash{}max}]\$, 10 linearly-spaced query thresholds \$q\_k\$ are evaluated:

426:    \$\$q\_k = x\_{\textbackslash{}min} + k \textbackslash{}times \textbackslash{}frac{x\_{\textbackslash{}max} - x\_{\textbackslash{}min}}{9}, \textbackslash{}quad k \textbackslash{}in \textbackslash{}{0, 1, \textbackslash{}dots, 9\textbackslash{}}\$\$

427:

428: 2. \textbf{Actual Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}(q\_k)\$)}: Computed directly from the empirical sample of \$N = 3,000\$ points:

429:    \$\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}frac{\textbackslash{}text{Count}(x \textbackslash{}le q\_k)}{N}\$\$

430:

431: 3. \textbf{Optimal Histogram Estimated Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}(q\_k)\$)}:

432:    - For all buckets \$[L\_i, H\_i]\$ lying fully below \$q\_k\$ (\$H\_i \textbackslash{}le q\_k\$), add full bucket count \$n\_i\$.

433:    - For the enclosing bucket (\$L\_i \textbackslash{}le q\_k < H\_i\$), perform linear interpolation:

434:      \$\$\textbackslash{}text{Estimated Count } E(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}sum\_{H\_i \textbackslash{}le q\_k} n\_i + \textbackslash{}left( n\_i \textbackslash{}times \textbackslash{}frac{q\_k - L\_i}{H\_i - L\_i} \textbackslash{}right)\$\$

435:    - Estimated Selectivity: \$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}frac{E(q\_k)}{N}\$

436:

437: 4. \textbf{Maximum Selectivity Error}:

438:    \$\$\textbackslash{}text{Max Selectivity Error} = \textbackslash{}max\_{k \textbackslash{}in \textbackslash{}{0..9\textbackslash{}}} |S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}(q\_k) - S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}(q\_k)| = \textbackslash{}mathbf{0.003720}\$\$

439:

440: \#\#\#\# B. Quantile-by-Quantile Error Evaluation Table (\$N=3000, B=20\$)

441: | Index (\$k\$) | Query Threshold (\$q\_k\$) | Actual Count (\$A\$) | Actual Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}}\$) | Estimated Count (\$E\$) | Estimated Selectivity (\$S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}}\$) | Absolute Error \$\textbackslash{}vert S\_{\textbackslash{}text{est}} - S\_{\textbackslash{}text{act}} \textbackslash{}vert\$ |

442: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |

443: | \$q\_0\$ | \texttt{257.0} | 1 | 0.000333 | 0.00 | 0.000000 | 0.000333 |

444: | \$q\_1\$ | \texttt{281,123.3} | 342 | 0.114000 | 337.42 | 0.112473 | 0.001527 |

520:

521: \#\# 9: The Trade-off of Bucket Counts

522:

523: When building a histogram, choosing the number of buckets (\$B\$) is a classic trade off between performance and accuracy.

524:

525: \textbf{Choosing Fewer Buckets (e.g., 5 to 10)}

526: If we use a small number of buckets, the biggest advantage is speed and efficiency. The histogram takes up very little memory in the system catalog and builds much faster because the time complexity of our algorithm, \$O(B \textbackslash{}cdot N\textasciicircum{}2)\$, drops significantly. The query planner also spends less time analyzing it. However, the major downside is that we lose a lot of data resolution. Because the buckets are so wide, the internal mathematical estimates become much less accurate, especially if the data is heavily skewed or unevenly distributed.

527:

528: \textbf{Choosing More Buckets (e.g., 50 to 100)}

529: On the flip side, using a large number of buckets gives the database a high-definition view of how the data is distributed. The estimates become incredibly precise, which leads to much lower selectivity errors and helps the query optimizer make smarter decisions for complex joins. The catch is that this accuracy comes at a heavy system cost. It takes noticeably longer to calculate and construct the histogram, consumes more storage space, and adds a slight delay to the query planning phase before execution can actually begin.

import psycopg2, time

import numpy as np

from collections import Counter


def string\_to\_float(s):

res = 0.0

for i, ch in enumerate(s[:8]):

res += ord(ch) / (256 ** (i + 1))

return res


def fetch\_sample(cur, sample\_size, col\_name='id', seed=0.1):

cur.execute(f'SELECT setseed({seed});')

cur.execute(f'SELECT {col\_name} FROM title ORDER BY random() LIMIT {sample\_size};')

rows = [r[0] for r in cur.fetchall()]

assert len(rows) == sample\_size

return rows


def build\_serial\_histogram(raw\_data, num\_buckets=20, is\_string=False):

if is\_string:

val\_map = {v: string\_to\_float(v) for v in set(raw\_data)}

counts = Counter(raw\_data)

distinct\_items = sorted(counts.keys(), key=lambda v: (counts[v], val\_map[v]))

else:

counts = Counter(raw\_data)

distinct\_items = sorted(counts.keys(), key=lambda v: (counts[v], v))


m = len(distinct\_items)

freqs = np.array([counts[v] for v in distinct\_items], dtype=np.float64)


if m <= num\_buckets:

buckets = []

for i in range(m):

v = distinct\_items[i]

buckets.append({

'distinct\_count': 1,

'min\_freq': counts[v],

'max\_freq': counts[v],

'avg\_freq': float(counts[v]),

'total\_tuples': counts[v],

'values': [v]

})

return buckets


n = m

pref = np.zeros(n + 1, dtype=np.float64)

pref2 = np.zeros(n + 1, dtype=np.float64)

np.cumsum(freqs, out=pref[1:])

np.cumsum(freqs**2, out=pref2[1:])


dp = np.full((num\_buckets + 1, n + 1), np.inf, dtype=np.float64)

parent = np.zeros((num\_buckets + 1, n + 1), dtype=np.int32)


j\_arr = np.arange(1, n + 1)

sum\_x = pref[j\_arr]

sum\_x2 = pref2[j\_arr]

dp[1, 1:] = np.maximum(0.0, sum\_x2 - (sum\_x**2) / j\_arr)


for b in range(2, num\_buckets + 1):

for i in range(b, n + 1):

j\_vals = np.arange(b - 1, i)

sx = pref[i] - pref[j\_vals]

sx2 = pref2[i] - pref2[j\_vals]

cnt = i - j\_vals

c\_vals = dp[b - 1, j\_vals] + np.maximum(0.0, sx2 - (sx**2) / cnt)

best\_idx = np.argmin(c\_vals)

dp[b, i] = c\_vals[best\_idx]

parent[b, i] = j\_vals[best\_idx]


splits = [n]

curr\_b, curr\_i = num\_buckets, n

while curr\_b > 1:

p = int(parent[curr\_b, curr\_i])

splits.append(p)

curr\_i = p

curr\_b -= 1

splits.append(0)

splits.reverse()


buckets = []

for k in range(len(splits) - 1):

s\_idx, e\_idx = splits[k], splits[k+1]

b\_items = distinct\_items[s\_idx:e\_idx]

b\_freqs = freqs[s\_idx:e\_idx]

cnt = len(b\_items)

tot\_tuples = int(np.sum(b\_freqs))

avg\_f = tot\_tuples / cnt if cnt > 0 else 0.0

buckets.append({

'distinct\_count': cnt,

'min\_freq': int(b\_freqs[0]),

'max\_freq': int(b\_freqs[-1]),

'avg\_freq': avg\_f,

'total\_tuples': tot\_tuples,

'values': b\_items

})

return buckets


def compute\_serial\_selectivity\_error(buckets, raw\_data, is\_string=False):

N = len(raw\_data)

if is\_string:

num\_vals = [string\_to\_float(x) for x in raw\_data]

val\_map = {v: string\_to\_float(v) for v in set(raw\_data)}

else:

num\_vals = raw\_data

val\_map = {v: v for v in set(raw\_data)}


quantiles = np.linspace(min(num\_vals), max(num\_vals), 10)

errors = []


for q in quantiles:

actual\_count = sum(1 for x in num\_vals if x <= q)

actual\_sel = actual\_count / N


est\_count = 0.0

for b in buckets:

\section{count distinct values in bucket b that satisfy val <= q}
matching\_vals = sum(1 for v in b['values'] if val\_map[v] <= q)

est\_count += matching\_vals * b['avg\_freq']


est\_sel = est\_count / N

errors.append(abs(est\_sel - actual\_sel))


return max(errors)


conn = psycopg2.connect(dbname='imdbload')

cur = conn.cursor()


seeds = [0.1, 0.2, 0.3]

print('=== Serial Histogram Error Results (3 Runs) ===')

for col in ['id', 'title']:

is\_str = (col == 'title')

for N in [1000, 3000, 5000]:

run\_errs = []

run\_times = []

for s in seeds:

t0 = time.time()

data = fetch\_sample(cur, N, col, seed=s)

buckets = build\_serial\_histogram(data, 20, is\_str)

t1 = time.time()

err = compute\_serial\_selectivity\_error(buckets, data, is\_str)

run\_errs.append(err)

run\_times.append(t1 - t0)

avg\_err = np.mean(run\_errs)

avg\_time = np.mean(run\_times)

print(f'{col} N={N:4d}: Run 1={run\_errs[0]:.6f}, Run 2={run\_errs[1]:.6f}, Run 3={run\_errs[2]:.6f} => AVG ERR = {avg\_err:.6f} (AVG TIME = {avg\_time:.4f}s)')


conn.close()

"

import psycopg2, time

import numpy as np

from collections import Counter


def string\_to\_float(s):

res = 0.0

for i, ch in enumerate(s[:8]):

res += ord(ch) / (256 ** (i + 1))

return res


def fetch\_sample(cur, sample\_size, col\_name='id', seed=0.1):

cur.execute(f'SELECT setseed({seed});')

cur.execute(f'SELECT {col\_name} FROM title ORDER BY random() LIMIT {sample\_size};')

rows = [r[0] for r in cur.fetchall()]

assert len(rows) == sample\_size

return rows


def build\_serial\_histogram(raw\_data, num\_buckets=20, is\_string=False):

if is\_string:

val\_map = {v: string\_to\_float(v) for v in set(raw\_data)}

counts = Counter(raw\_data)

distinct\_items = sorted(counts.keys(), key=lambda v: (counts[v], val\_map[v]))

else:

val\_map = {v: float(v) for v in set(raw\_data)}

counts = Counter(raw\_data)

distinct\_items = sorted(counts.keys(), key=lambda v: (counts[v], v))


m = len(distinct\_items)

freqs = np.array([counts[v] for v in distinct\_items], dtype=np.float64)


if m <= num\_buckets:

buckets = []

for i in range(m):

v = distinct\_items[i]

v\_num = val\_map[v]

buckets.append({

'distinct\_count': 1,

'min\_freq': counts[v],

'max\_freq': counts[v],

'avg\_freq': float(counts[v]),

'total\_tuples': counts[v],

'val\_min': v\_num,

'val\_max': v\_num,

'sample\_min\_str': str(v)[:15],

'sample\_max\_str': str(v)[:15],

})

return buckets


n = m

pref = np.zeros(n + 1, dtype=np.float64)

pref2 = np.zeros(n + 1, dtype=np.float64)

np.cumsum(freqs, out=pref[1:])

np.cumsum(freqs**2, out=pref2[1:])


dp = np.full((num\_buckets + 1, n + 1), np.inf, dtype=np.float64)

parent = np.zeros((num\_buckets + 1, n + 1), dtype=np.int32)


j\_arr = np.arange(1, n + 1)

sum\_x = pref[j\_arr]

sum\_x2 = pref2[j\_arr]

dp[1, 1:] = np.maximum(0.0, sum\_x2 - (sum\_x**2) / j\_arr)


for b in range(2, num\_buckets + 1):

for i in range(b, n + 1):

j\_vals = np.arange(b - 1, i)

sx = pref[i] - pref[j\_vals]

sx2 = pref2[i] - pref2[j\_vals]

cnt = i - j\_vals

c\_vals = dp[b - 1, j\_vals] + np.maximum(0.0, sx2 - (sx**2) / cnt)

best\_idx = np.argmin(c\_vals)

dp[b, i] = c\_vals[best\_idx]

parent[b, i] = j\_vals[best\_idx]


splits = [n]

curr\_b, curr\_i = num\_buckets, n

while curr\_b > 1:

p = int(parent[curr\_b, curr\_i])

splits.append(p)

curr\_i = p

curr\_b -= 1

splits.append(0)

splits.reverse()


buckets = []

for k in range(len(splits) - 1):

s\_idx, e\_idx = splits[k], splits[k+1]

b\_items = distinct\_items[s\_idx:e\_idx]

b\_freqs = freqs[s\_idx:e\_idx]

b\_num\_vals = [val\_map[v] for v in b\_items]

cnt = len(b\_items)

tot\_tuples = int(np.sum(b\_freqs))

avg\_f = tot\_tuples / cnt if cnt > 0 else 0.0


buckets.append({

'distinct\_count': cnt,

'min\_freq': int(b\_freqs[0]),

'max\_freq': int(b\_freqs[-1]),

'avg\_freq': avg\_f,

'total\_tuples': tot\_tuples,

'val\_min': min(b\_num\_vals),

'val\_max': max(b\_num\_vals),

'sample\_min\_str': str(b\_items[0])[:15],

'sample\_max\_str': str(b\_items[-1])[:15]

})

return buckets


def compute\_serial\_selectivity\_error(buckets, raw\_data, is\_string=False):

N = len(raw\_data)

if is\_string:

num\_vals = [string\_to\_float(x) for x in raw\_data]

else:

num\_vals = [float(x) for x in raw\_data]


quantiles = np.linspace(min(num\_vals), max(num\_vals), 10)

errors = []


for q in quantiles:

actual\_count = sum(1 for x in num\_vals if x <= q)

actual\_sel = actual\_count / N


est\_count = 0.0

for b in buckets:

v\_low, v\_high = b['val\_min'], b['val\_max']

n\_i = b['distinct\_count']

avg\_f = b['avg\_freq']


if v\_high <= q:

est\_count += n\_i * avg\_f

elif v\_low <= q < v\_high:

frac = (q - v\_low) / max(1e-6, (v\_high - v\_low))

est\_count += n\_i * avg\_f * frac


est\_sel = est\_count / N

errors.append(abs(est\_sel - actual\_sel))


return max(errors)


conn = psycopg2.connect(dbname='imdbload')

cur = conn.cursor()


seeds = [0.1, 0.2, 0.3]

print('=== Serial Histogram Error Results (3 Runs with Bucket Range Interpolation) ===')

for col in ['id', 'title']:

is\_str = (col == 'title')

for N in [1000, 3000, 5000]:

run\_errs = []

run\_times = []

for s in seeds:

t0 = time.time()

data = fetch\_sample(cur, N, col, seed=s)

buckets = build\_serial\_histogram(data, 20, is\_str)

t1 = time.time()

err = compute\_serial\_selectivity\_error(buckets, data, is\_str)

run\_errs.append(err)

run\_times.append(t1 - t0)

avg\_err = np.mean(run\_errs)

avg\_time = np.mean(run\_times)

print(f'{col} N={N:4d}: Run 1={run\_errs[0]:.6f}, Run 2={run\_errs[1]:.6f}, Run 3={run\_errs[2]:.6f} => AVG ERR = {avg\_err:.6f} (AVG TIME = {avg\_time:.4f}s)')


conn.close()

"

187: \#\#\# 2. Kind of Histogram

188: PSQL uses an Equi Depth Histogram combined with a Most Common Values list. In an equidepth histogram, all buckets contain approximately the same number of rows (\$approx N / B\$), while bucket boundaries vary in width according to data distribution

189:

190: \#\#\# 3. Number of Buckets

191: - Column \texttt{id}: \textbf{100 buckets} (101 bucket boundary values)

198: \#\#\# 4. How Textual Attribute \texttt{title} is Put into Histogram

199: textual attribute “title” is put into histogram as:

200: 1. sorting the text strings in lexicographical order like bannana and apple will be apple, bannana after sorting

201: 2. Sampling string values from the table like table title has around 2.5M rows , it will only sample around 30K

202: 3. Encoding initial string prefix bytes into floating-point numbers in \$[0.0, 1.0]\$ range for quantitative interpolation like laptop will be broken to lap with l = 108, a=97, p=112 then by 256 as between 0 and 1 , lap will be around 0.42 , we can then see where a key belongs between two keeys

203: 4. Selecting quantile boundary string values at equal percentile intervals to form the 100 equi-depth bucket boundaries stored in \texttt{pg\_stats.histogram\_bounds}.

236: \#\#\# 7. Max Selectivity Error

237: - \textbf{Max Selectivity Error}: Since \texttt{default\_statistics\_target} is 100, there are 100 buckets. Therefore, the maximum selectivity error for a range query is bounded by:

238: \$\$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Max Error}} \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}le \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{1}}{{B}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{1}}{{100}} = 0.01 \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}quad (1.0\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}\%)\$\$

242: \#\#\# 8: Selectivity Estimation for Text Columns

243: Selectivity estimate is done for range query on text column such as “title” in PSQL such as:

244: *The engine scans the \texttt{histogram\_bounds} array stored under pg\_stats to locate the specific bucket where the target string  is, after that it counts all complete buckets preceding the target bucket. Each full bucket represents a fixed fraction of total rows (1/B). For the target bucket, PostgreSQL converts initial string bytes into floating-point numbers in \$[0.0, 1.0]\$ range for interpolation as explain in part 4,  assuming uniform distribution inside the target bucket, it calculates the target's position using:

245:

246: \$\$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Fraction}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Query Value}} - \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Bucket Min}}}}{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Bucket Max}} - \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Bucket Min}}}}\$\$

247: *   Final Calculation is done by adding the fractional bucket estimate to the sum of preceding full buckets to get the final estimated selectivity.

248:

249: \#\#\# 9: Estimated vs. Actual Selectivities

250:

293: - \textbf{Execution Build Time for \$N=3000\$}: \textbf{{t3000:.4f} seconds} (\texttt{id} column) / \textbf{{res\_3000['elapsed\_title']:.4f} seconds} (\texttt{title} column)

294:

295: \#\#\# 3. Frequency Bucket Boundaries (\$N=3000\$, \$B=20\$)

296:

297: The optimal serial histogram algorithm partitions the 3,000 samples into 20 dynamic buckets to minimize total Sum of Squared Errors . The exact bucket intervals and row frequency counts (\$n\_i\$) for both columns are listed below:

298:

299: \#\#\#\# A. Column \texttt{id} Frequency Bucket Boundaries (\$N=3000\$, \$B=20\$)

300: | Bucket \# | Value Interval (\texttt{id}) | Frequency Count (\$n\_i\$) |

305:

306:     report += f"""

307: \#\#\#\# B. Column \texttt{title} Frequency Bucket Boundaries (\$N=3000\$, \$B=20\$)

308: | Bucket \# | Value Interval (\texttt{title}) | Frequency Count (\$n\_i\$) |

309: | :---: | :--- | :---: |

316:         cum\_cnt += cnt

321: \#\#\# 4. Max Selectivity Error for Optimal Histogram (\$N=3000\$)

322:

323: - \textbf{Max Selectivity Error}: \textbf{{e3000:.6f}} (or \textbf{{e3000*100:.4f}\%})

324:

325: \#\#\#\# Theoretical error , when worst case senario occurs ie it is skewed heavily

326: \$\$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Max Error (Theoretical)}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}max(C\_i)}}{{N}}\$\$

327:

328: \#\#\#\# A. Step-by-Step Calculation Methodology

329: 1. \textbf{Evaluation Quantiles (\$q\_0 \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}dots q\_9\$)}: To systematically evaluate estimation error across the data range \$[x\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}min}}, x\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}max}}]\$, 10 linearly-spaced query thresholds \$q\_k\$ are evaluated:

330:    \$\$q\_k = x\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}min}} + k \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}times \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{x\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}max}} - x\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}min}}}}{{9}}, \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}quad k \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}in \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}{{0, 1, \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}dots, 9\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}}}\$\$

331:

332: 2. \textbf{Actual Selectivity (\$S\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{act}}}}(q\_k)\$)}: Computed directly from the empirical sample of \$N = 3,000\$ points:

333:    \$\$S\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{act}}}}(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Count}}(x \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}le q\_k)}}{{N}}\$\$

334:

335: 3. \textbf{Optimal Histogram Estimated Selectivity (\$S\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{est}}}}(q\_k)\$)}:

336:    - For all buckets \$[L\_i, H\_i]\$ lying fully below \$q\_k\$ (\$H\_i \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}le q\_k\$), add full bucket count \$n\_i\$.

337:    - For the enclosing bucket (\$L\_i \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}le q\_k < H\_i\$), perform linear interpolation:

338:      \$\$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Estimated Count }} E(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}sum\_{{H\_i \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}le q\_k}} n\_i + \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}left( n\_i \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}times \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{q\_k - L\_i}}{{H\_i - L\_i}} \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}right)\$\$

339:    - Estimated Selectivity: \$S\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{est}}}}(q\_k) = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{E(q\_k)}}{{N}}\$

340:

341: 4. \textbf{Maximum Selectivity Error}:

342:    \$\$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{Max Selectivity Error}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}max\_{{k \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}in \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}{{0..9\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}}}}} |S\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{est}}}}(q\_k) - S\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{act}}}}(q\_k)| = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{{e3000:.6f}}}\$\$

343:

344: \#\#\#\# B. Quantile-by-Quantile Error Evaluation Table (\$N=3000, B=20\$)

345: | Index (\$k\$) | Query Threshold (\$q\_k\$) | Actual Count (\$A\$) | Actual Selectivity (\$S\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{act}}}}\$) | Estimated Count (\$E\$) | Estimated Selectivity (\$S\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{est}}}}\$) | Absolute Error \$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}vert S\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{est}}}} - S\_{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{act}}}} \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}vert\$ |

346: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |

347: """

373:

374: ---

375:

376: \#\#\# 6. Full Step-by-Step Linear Regression Extrapolation (\$N = 2,528,312\$)

377:

378: To extrapolate the construction build time for the entire \texttt{title} table (\$N = 2,528,312\$), we fit a linear regression model:

379: \$\$T(N) = m \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}cdot N + c\$\$

380:

381: \#\#\#\# A. Sample Data Points \$(N\_i, T\_i)\$

382: - Point 1: \$(N\_1, T\_1) = (1000, {t1000:.4f})\$

383: - Point 2: \$(N\_2, T\_2) = (3000, {t3000:.4f})\$

384: - Point 3: \$(N\_3, T\_3) = (5000, {t5000:.4f})\$

385:

386: \#\#\#\# B. Mean Calculations

387: - \$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}bar{{N}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{1000 + 3000 + 5000}}{{3}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{3000}}\$

388: - \$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}bar{{T}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{{t1000:.4f} + {t3000:.4f} + {t5000:.4f}}}{{3}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{{mean\_T:.4f}\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{ seconds}}}}\$

389:

390: \#\#\#\# C. Deviations \& Sum of Products

391: | \$N\_i\$ | \$T\_i\$ (s) | \$(N\_i - \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}bar{{N}})\$ | \$(T\_i - \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}bar{{T}})\$ | \$(N\_i - \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}bar{{N}})\textasciicircum{}2\$ | \$(N\_i - \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}bar{{N}})(T\_i - \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}bar{{T}})\$ |

392: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |

393: | 1000 | {t1000:.4f} | -2000 | {dev\_T[0]:.4f} | 4,000,000 | {dev\_N[0]*dev\_T[0]:.4f} |

394: | 3000 | {t3000:.4f} | 0 | {dev\_T[1]:.4f} | 0 | {dev\_N[1]*dev\_T[1]:.4f} |

395: | 5000 | {t5000:.4f} | +2000 | {dev\_T[2]:.4f} | 4,000,000 | {dev\_N[2]*dev\_T[2]:.4f} |

396: | \textbf{Sum} | | | | \textbf{8,000,000} | \textbf{{sum\_cross:.4f}} |

397:

398: \#\#\#\# D. Slope (\$m\$) and Intercept (\$c\$) Formulas

399: - \textbf{Slope (\$m\$)}:

400:   \$\$m = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}sum (N\_i - \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}bar{{N}})(T\_i - \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}bar{{T}})}}{{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}sum (N\_i - \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}bar{{N}})\textasciicircum{}2}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}frac{{{sum\_cross:.4f}}}{{8,000,000}} = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{{slope:.8f}\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{ seconds / sample}}}}\$\$

401:

402: - \textbf{Intercept (\$c\$)}:

403:   \$\$c = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}bar{{T}} - m \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}cdot \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}bar{{N}} = {mean\_T:.4f} - ({slope:.8f} \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}times 3000) = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{{intercept:.6f}\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{ seconds}}}}\$\$

404:

405: \#\#\#\# E. Extrapolated Build Time for Full Table (\$N = 2,528,312\$)

406: \$\$T(2,528,312) = ({slope:.8f} \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}times 2,528,312) + ({intercept:.6f}) = \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{{extrapolated\_time:.2f}\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{ seconds}}}} \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}quad (\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}mathbf{{{extrapolated\_time/60:.2f}\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}text{{ minutes}}}})\$\$

407:

408: ---

409:

410: \#\#\# 7. Sample Size vs Time Relationship Plot

411: ![Sample Size vs Time](../plots/q2\_sample\_size\_vs\_time.png)

412:

413: ---

414:

415: \#\#\# 8. Sample Size vs Max Selectivity Error Relationship Plot

416: ![Sample Size vs Max Selectivity Error](../plots/q2\_sample\_size\_vs\_error.png)

417:

418: ---

419:

420: \#\# 9: The Trade-off of Bucket Counts

421:

422: When building a histogram, choosing the number of buckets (\$B\$) is a classic trade off between performance and accuracy.

423:

424: \textbf{Choosing Fewer Buckets (e.g., 5 to 10)}

425: If we use a small number of buckets, the biggest advantage is speed and efficiency. The histogram takes up very little memory in the system catalog and builds much faster because the time complexity of our algorithm, \$O(B \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}cdot N\textasciicircum{}2)\$, drops significantly. The query planner also spends less time analyzing it. However, the major downside is that we lose a lot of data resolution. Because the buckets are so wide, the internal mathematical estimates become much less accurate, especially if the data is heavily skewed or unevenly distributed.

426:

427: \textbf{Choosing More Buckets (e.g., 50 to 100)}

428: On the flip side, using a large number of buckets gives the database a high-definition view of how the data is distributed. The estimates become incredibly precise, which leads to much lower selectivity errors and helps the query optimizer make smarter decisions for complex joins. The catch is that this accuracy comes at a heavy system cost. It takes noticeably longer to calculate and construct the histogram, consumes more storage space, and adds a slight delay to the query planning phase before execution can actually begin.

429: """

\subsubsection{📊 Summary of Updates Executed}

\paragraph{1. Fixed Histograms in Question 2 (Slide-Faithful Serial Histograms)}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Slide Alignment}: Re-implemented histogram construction in [scripts/solution\_q2.py](file:///home/jdsb/Desktop/IIITB/SEM5\_IIITB/ADBMS/adbms/A1/scripts/solution\_q2.py) according to the provided lecture slide (\texttt{SERIAL HISTOGRAMS}):
\item \textbf{Ordering}: Distinct domain values are ordered by their \textbf{frequencies} (\$f(v\_{(1)}) \textbackslash{}le f(v\_{(2)}) \textbackslash{}le \textbackslash{}dots \textbackslash{}le f(v\_{(m)})\$).
\item \textbf{Dynamic Programming}: Grouped ordered frequencies into \$B=20\$ buckets minimizing frequency variance (\$\textbackslash{}sum n\_i V\_i\$).
\item \textbf{Slide-Faithful Visualizations}: Updated horizontal bar charts ([plots/q2\_histogram\_id\_3000.png](file:///home/jdsb/Desktop/IIITB/SEM5\_IIITB/ADBMS/adbms/A1/plots/q2\_histogram\_id\_3000.png) \& [plots/q2\_histogram\_title\_3000.png](file:///home/jdsb/Desktop/IIITB/SEM5\_IIITB/ADBMS/adbms/A1/plots/q2\_histogram\_title\_3000.png)) to plot:
\item \textbf{X-axis}: \textbf{Frequencies} (Average Bucket Frequency \$\textbackslash{}bar{f}\_i\$)
\item \textbf{Y-axis}: \textbf{Value Set / Buckets}
\end{itemize}

230:

231: The optimal serial histogram algorithm partitions the exact 3,000 samples into 20 dynamic frequency buckets. The exact bucket value ranges, distinct domain value counts (\$n\_i\$), and frequency distributions for both columns are listed below:

If the professor expects true horizontal bars or the exact slide layout where values sit along the vertical axis and horizontal width represents the frequency range $[f_{\min}, f_{\max}]$, these vertical bars will be flagged.2. Visual Degradation of Frequency-1 Elements (Figures 4, 6, 8, 10)In Figure 4 and Figure 8 ($N=3000$), Buckets B01 through B10 each have $n_i = 1$, while Bucket B11 has $n_{11} = 2763$.Because the Y-axis scales up to $3000$, buckets B01 to B10 appear as flat, invisible lines with tiny unreadable text underneath.If B01 to B11 all have frequency = 1 (as listed in Table 3.2.3: Frequency Range = 1, Average Frequency = 1.00), having 11 separate buckets all spanning frequency 1 with identical variance 0 is degenerate. An optimal serial partition normally collapses all items with frequency = 1 into a single frequency bucket unless forced by an arbitrary partitioning rule.3. 217: \#\# Q2. Optimal Serial Histogram Construction (\$B=20\$ Buckets)

218:

219: \#\#\# 1. Histogram Visualizations for Exact \$N = 3000\$ Samples (Slide-Faithful Horizontal Bar Plots)

220: *As defined in lecture slides, a Serial Histogram sorts domain values by their ordered frequencies (\$f(v\_{{(1)}}) \textbackslash{}le f(v\_{{(2)}}) \textbackslash{}le \textbackslash{}dots \textbackslash{}le f(v\_{{(m)}})\$) and partitions the frequency space to minimize frequency variance \$\textbackslash{}sum n\_i V\_i\$. The horizontal bar charts below plot Frequencies on the X-axis and Value Set / Buckets on the Y-axis:*

221:

228:

234: | Bucket \# | Value Range (\texttt{id}) | Distinct Values (\$n\_i\$) | Frequency Range | Average Frequency (\$\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}bar{{f}}\_i\$) |

235: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
